439	\label{tab:seed_replication}
440	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
441	\toprule
442	Condition & Acc (\%) & $\Delta$ (pp) \\
443	\midrule
444	Within-seed (seed=42) & 78.20 & --- \\
445	Within-seed (seed=43) & 77.15 & $-$1.05$^\dagger$ \\
446	\midrule
447	Cross-seed (train 42$\to$test 43) & 77.85 & \\
448	Cross-seed (train 43$\to$test 42) & 75.68 & \\
449	Cross-seed avg & 76.76 & $-$1.30$^\ddagger$ \\
450	\bottomrule
451	\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\footnotesize $^\dagger$vs.\ baseline (78.20\%). $^\ddagger$vs.\ within-seed avg (78.07\%).}
452	\end{tabular}
453	\end{table}
454	
455	The seed=43 LoRA achieves 77.15\% (95\% CI [76.4, 78.15]), within 1.05\,pp of the seed=42 baseline.
456	Cross-seed transfer averages 76.76\%, a 1.30\,pp deficit relative to within-seed performance.
457	Top-5 feature importance is identical across seeds (\texttt{var\_surprisal}, \texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{mean\_ppl}, \texttt{var\_ppl}, \texttt{p75\_ppl}), confirming that depth signal arises from the recursive process itself rather than idiosyncratic LoRA weight configurations.
458	
459	\subsection{Generation Length Ablation}
460	\label{app:length_ablation}
461	
462	Table~\ref{tab:length_ablation} reports classification accuracy as a function of passage length (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, depths 0--3).
463	
464	\begin{table}[h]
465	\centering
466	\small
467	\caption{Generation length ablation (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, 5-fold CV). Accuracy increases monotonically with passage length; the 9.47\,pp spread confirms that longer passages provide stronger statistical signal.}
468	\label{tab:length_ablation}
469	\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
470	\toprule
471	Passage length (tokens) & Acc (\%) \\
472	\midrule
473	128 & 68.35 \\
474	256 & 73.80 \\
475	512 & 77.82 \\
476	\bottomrule
477	\end{tabular}
478	\end{table}
479	
480	Depth signal is detectable from 128 tokens (2.7$\times$ chance) and saturates near 500 tokens.
481	The monotonic increase reflects the statistical nature of the features: longer passages yield more stable estimates of perplexity percentiles and surprisal variance, reducing within-class overlap at adjacent depth boundaries.
482	
483	\subsection{Mixed-Depth Corpus}
484	\label{app:mixed_depth}
485	
486	Per-passage classification is largely independent of corpus composition: evaluating on a corpus containing mixed proportions of d0--d3 passages (rather than balanced classes) yields macro-recall $\Delta{=}{-}1.62$\,pp relative to the balanced benchmark (within the 2\,pp tolerance).
487	Individual class deltas range from $-$0.45\,pp to $-$2.43\,pp (d1 shows the largest shift), confirming that the RF classifier operates on per-sample features without sensitivity to the class distribution of the evaluation corpus.
488	
489	\subsection{Extended Discussion}
490	\label{app:extended_discussion}
491	
492	\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}
493	Current data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.
494	Recursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.
495	The 70--85\% in-distribution accuracy and $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest depth-graduated filtering is feasible within known model--strategy configurations, though the d1/d2 boundary inversion under cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) means reliable curation requires either known decoding strategy metadata or an upstream strategy-identification step; a pilot study (Appendix~\ref{app:curation_poc}) yields an inconclusive RF-vs-unfiltered comparison ($p>0.4$), though RF-filtered subsets significantly outperform size-matched random subsampling ($p<0.001$--$0.02$); large-scale validation remains open.
496	Importance weighting \cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.
497	
498	\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}
499	Depth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.
500	Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.
501	Source attribution \cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.
502	Depth estimation characterizes \emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.
503	A layered provenance system combining these three signals---watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth---could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.
504	
```

the single SHAP exception is GPT-2~XL top-$k$, where \\textsc{p75\\_ppl} displaces \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} to rank 3).\nKendall's $W{=}0.90$ across all three methods (Gini, permutation, SHAP) indicates strong concordance ($p{<}0.005$).\nPairwise Spearman correlations are: SHAP--Gini $\\rho{=}0.93$, SHAP--permutation $\\rho{=}0.84$, Gini--permutation $\\rho{=}0.76$.\nPer-cell top-5 overlap between SHAP and permutation importance is 3--4 out of 5 features; the deficit is most pronounced under top-$k$ sampling (46--53\\%), where vocabulary truncation removes the tail tokens that carry depth-discriminative signal.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-class recall (\\%) for all 9 atlas cells. d2 is the lowest-recall class in every cell. All experiments use Pythia-1.4B nucleus data. RF baseline: 78.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dcol_iterations}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\nVer. & Backbone & Config & Outcome \\\\\n\\midrule\nv1 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse (31.5\\%) \\\\\nv2 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & OOM \\\\\nv3 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & Forgetting + OOM \\\\\nv4 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse \\\\\nv5 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & NaN gradients \\\\\nv6 & RoBERTa & FT, grad clip 1.0 & Stable; 64.5\\% ($-$13.7pp) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Zero-Shot LLM Prompt Template}\n\\label{app:zeroshot_prompt}\n\nBoth DeepSeek V3.2 and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct use identical prompting.\nThe system message defines recursive generation depth with per-depth descriptions (depth~0: human-written; depth~1--3: successive LLM generation cycles) and lists expected textual patterns at each depth (vocabulary richness, repetition, coherence).\nThe user message presents the passage (truncated to ${\\approx}$384 words) and requests a JSON response containing an integer depth prediction (0--3), a confidence score, and a one-sentence reasoning.\nNo in-context examples are provided.\n\n\\begin{quote}\n\\small\n\\textbf{System:} ``You are an expert at detecting AI-generated text and estimating its generation depth. `Recursive generation depth' refers to how many times a text has been through an LLM generation cycle: Depth~0: Original human-written text [\\ldots] Depth~3: Text generated by an LLM given depth-2 text as input. Key patterns by depth: Depth~0 (human): Rich vocabulary [\\ldots] Depth~3 (third-gen): Noticeable repetition, formulaic structure [\\ldots]''\n\n\\textbf{User:} ``Analyze the following text and estimate its recursive generation depth (0, 1, 2, or 3). TEXT: \\{text\\}. Respond with ONLY a JSON object: \\{\\texttt{\"depth\"}: $\\langle$int 0--3$\\rangle$, \\texttt{\"confidence\"}: $\\langle$float 0--1$\\rangle$, \\texttt{\"reasoning\"}: $\\langle$str$\\rangle$\\}''\n\\end{quote}\n\n\\subsection{Temperature Robustness}\n\\label{app:multi_tau}\n\nThe cross-strategy results in \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy} use a single temperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$).\nTo test whether depth signal generalizes across temperatures, we repeat the classification pipeline at $\\tau \\in \\{0.5, 0.7, 0.9, 1.1\\}$ on Pythia-1.4B.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-$\\tau$ robustness.}\nFor $\\tau \\in [0.5, 0.9]$, d1-vs-d2 AUC exceeds 0.80 (peak 0.851 at $\\tau{=}0.7$, comparable to nucleus $p{=}0.95$'s 0.861) and 4-class accuracy ranges from 75.4\\% to 77.3\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:multi_tau_within}), confirming the depth signal is not an artifact of a single temperature.\nThe same top-5 features (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}, \\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_surprisal}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}) dominate across all $\\tau$ values with only minor rank-order variation.\n\n\\paragraph{Collapse at $\\tau{=}1.1$: memorization as prerequisite.}\nAt $\\tau{=}1.1$, 4-class accuracy drops to 57.2\\% and d1v2 AUC falls to 0.528.\nTraining loss reveals the mechanism: at low temperature ($\\tau{=}0.5$), fine-tuning loss decreases steeply across recursive depths ($2.27 \\to 0.42 \\to 0.16$), indicating rapid memorization of the model's own output.\nAt $\\tau{=}1.1$, loss remains nearly flat ($2.27 \\to 1.91 \\to 1.85$)---high-entropy output resists memorization, so depth-dependent distributional shifts do not accumulate.\nThis establishes \\emph{memorization as a prerequisite} for detectable depth signal: recursive depth is discriminable only when successive fine-tuning iterations progressively reshape the output distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-$\\tau$ non-transferability.}\nLeave-one-$\\tau$-out transfer yields mean accuracy of 46.7\\% with mean d1v2 AUC of 0.319 (Table~\\ref{tab:multi_tau_cross})---worse than cross-strategy transfer (59.1\\%; \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).\nHoldout $\\tau{=}0.5$ produces inverted d1v2 AUC (0.176), confirming that different temperatures produce surprisal distributions with incompatible shapes.\nThis extends the strategy-specificity finding: even within a single decoding family, the feature space is temperature-specific, reinforcing the need for strategy-conditioned classifiers.\n\n\\paragraph{Ecological validity.}\nOn WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages), all three model-specific RF classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy despite $+$621\\% domain shift in mean perplexity---partly an easy case, as WikiText's higher perplexity places it farther from d1--d3 in feature space.\nOn noisier web-crawl data (5{,}000 C4 passages), FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL), concentrated in template/SEO content with high n-gram repetition (Table~\\ref{tab:calibration}); deployment on heterogeneous web data requires model-specific calibration.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Within-$\\tau$ 4-class classification (Pythia-1.4B, 5-fold CV). d$i$v$j$: pairwise AUC for depth $i$ vs.\\ depth $j$.}\n\\label{tab:multi_tau_within}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$\\tau$ & 4-class acc (\\%) & d1v2 AUC & d0v1 AUC & d2v3 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.5 & 77.3 & 0.842 & 0.996 & 0.764 \\\\\n0.7 & 75.4 & 0.851 & 0.997 & 0.720 \\\\\n0.9 & 75.9 & 0.808 & 0.991 & 0.781 \\\\\n1.1 & 57.2 & 0.528 & 0.988 & 0.599 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-$\\tau$ leave-one-out transfer (Pythia-1.4B). Train on three temperatures, test on the held-out one.}\n\\label{tab:multi_tau_cross}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc (\\%) & d1v2 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\tau{=}0.5$ & 52.2 & 0.176 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}0.7$ & 41.0 & 0.299 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}0.9$ & 46.4 & 0.277 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}1.1$ & 47.1 & 0.525 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 46.7 & 0.319 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}\n\\label{app:feature_overlap}\n\nWe probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features via linear probing of [CLS] representations from DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base.\nPLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics---\\textsc{var\\_surprisal} and \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing, indicating these features are not linearly accessible in the pretrained representation space.\nThis confirms that the 19pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\\S\\ref{app:e2e}) stems from complementary---not redundant---representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the targeted statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{app:e2e}\n\nDeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} finetuned end-to-end achieve 58.3\\% ($\\pm$4.7\\%) and 59.5\\% ($\\pm$3.2\\%) respectively---lagging the RF baseline (78.2\\%) by 19.9pp and 18.7pp.\nThe 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture bias; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e_detail} shows both backbones share the same failure mode---high d0 F1 ($>$0.94) but collapsed d1/d2 performance (F1 $\\leq$ 0.42)---while RF achieves 0.596 d2 recall, a $>$2$\\times$ advantage at the hardest depth class.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-class performance for end-to-end PLM classifiers vs.\\ RF baseline (Pythia-1.4B nucleus). PLM columns report F1; RF d2 column reports recall (the class with the largest PLM--RF gap). Both PLM backbones share the same failure pattern: collapsed d1/d2 scores despite adequate d0/d3.}\n\\label{tab:e2e_detail}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Acc (\\%) & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeBERTa-v3 & 58.3\\,$\\pm$\\,4.7 & .947 & .378 & .247 & .656 \\\\\nRoBERTa-base & 59.5\\,$\\pm$\\,3.2 & .958 & .416 & .261 & .647 \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 feat.) & 78.2 & --- & --- & .596$^\\dagger$ & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Recall; shown for the depth class with the largest PLM--RF gap.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}\nSHAP TreeExplainer analysis confirms that perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\\% of RF's total contribution.\nThree independent importance measures---SHAP, Gini, and permutation---show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.93$, SHAP--permutation $0.84$; \\S\\ref{app:permutation}), with \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9.\nLinear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (\\S\\ref{app:feature_overlap}), indicating that pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics---a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\\% (\\S\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) underperform feature-based methods, suggesting depth estimation benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.\nThis comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.\n\n\\subsection{Domain Bias Analysis}\n\\label{app:domain_bias}\n\nTo assess whether the classifier exploits depth-related features or memorizes stylistic cues from individual seed articles, we conduct three sub-experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological note.}\nAn initial implementation assigned sample IDs with a global counter across depths, inadvertently correlating seed-group identity with depth (LOGO accuracy 64.0\\%, seed-group prediction 20.8\\%).\nWe discovered the issue during internal review, corrected to per-depth seed assignment ensuring each group contains equal samples per depth, and re-ran all sub-experiments.\nThe results below use the corrected pipeline; the initial (buggy) numbers are reported for transparency.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-A: Standard CV.}\nFive-fold CV accuracy on 3-class (d1/d2/d3) classification ranges from 59\\% to 82\\% across the 9 atlas cells (up to 2.5$\\times$ above the 33.3\\% chance level), confirming that depth signal is the dominant classification factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-B: LOGO evaluation.}\nWe assign each passage to one of 10 seed-article groups (balanced: each group contains equal samples per depth) and perform leave-one-group-out CV.\nLOGO mean accuracy is 72.1\\% (range 59.5--81.6\\%), matching standard CV (72.0\\%) within noise (before fix: 64.0\\%, reflecting the depth--group confound).\nPer-cell fold standard deviations range from 0.4pp to 1.4pp, indicating that cross-group generalization is as reliable as within-group classification.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-C: Negative control.}\nWe train two classifiers on identical features: one predicting depth, the other predicting seed-group identity (10-class, chance = 10\\%).\nDepth prediction accuracy averages 72.0\\%; seed-group prediction averages 10.2\\%---indistinguishable from chance (before fix: 20.8\\%, inflated by the depth--group confound).\nThe depth-to-seed-group accuracy ratio is 7.1$\\times$, confirming that the feature representation encodes depth but not seed-article identity.\n\nTaken together, these results show that seed-article grouping has no measurable effect on depth estimation after correcting the group assignment: LOGO accuracy equals standard CV, and seed-group prediction is at chance.\n\n\\subsection{Wild-Data Calibration}\n\\label{app:calibration}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Wild-data d0 accuracy and FPR for nucleus RF classifiers. All passages are human-written (true d0). FPR = fraction misclassified as d1+. 95\\% Wilson CIs computed for C4 ($n{=}5{,}000$).}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Source & $n$ & d0 (\\%) & FPR (\\%) & 95\\% Wilson CI \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B & WikiText & 546 & 100.0 & 0.0 & --- \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 79.8 & 20.2 & [19.1, 21.3] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nGPT-2~XL & WikiText & 546 & 99.3 & 0.7 & --- \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 77.6 & 22.4 & [21.3, 23.6] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nOLMo-1B & WikiText & 546 & 98.5 & 1.5 & --- \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 95.3 & 4.7 & [4.2, 5.3] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn WikiText-103, all three classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, confirming that editorial text is reliably distinguished from recursive generations across model families.\nOn C4, FPR varies sharply: OLMo (4.7\\%) vs.\\ Pythia (20.2\\%) and GPT-2~XL (22.4\\%).\nC4 misclassifications concentrate in template and SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0 passages), rather than in random noise.\nThe model gap likely reflects training data composition: OLMo (trained on Dolma, which includes diverse web data) produces d0 features more tolerant of web-crawl variation, while Pythia and GPT-2~XL (trained primarily on Wikipedia/WebText) map template-heavy content into the d1+ region.\nAmong C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\\%), d2 (3.7\\%), d3 (9.6\\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\\%), d2 (0.5\\%), d3 (1.3\\%)---the non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Curation PoC}\n\\label{app:curation_poc}\n\nTo validate that depth estimation translates to actionable data curation, we conduct a proof-of-concept downstream evaluation.\nWe fine-tune Pythia-410M with LoRA ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$) for 3 epochs on 8 data conditions derived from the Pythia nucleus depth-estimation corpus, repeating each condition over 5 random seeds.\nConditions vary the selection mechanism (RF-predicted depth, oracle depth, random subsampling) and stringency (strict $\\approx$25\\%, moderate $\\approx$50\\%, soft threshold).\nTable~\\ref{tab:curation_poc} reports per-condition perplexity; Table~\\ref{tab:curation_comparisons} reports pairwise contrasts.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Curation PoC: per-condition perplexity (Pythia-410M LoRA, 5 seeds). $\\Delta$ is signed so that positive values indicate lower (better) perplexity than unfiltered. $^*$\\,: $p<0.05$ (paired $t$-test vs.\\ unfiltered).}\n\\label{tab:curation_poc}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Mean PPL & Std & $n$ & $\\Delta$ vs Unfilt. & $p$ & $d_z$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nunfiltered        & 15.776 & 0.066 & 10{,}000 & ---      & ---   & ---      \\\\\nrf\\_soft          & 15.733 & 0.038 &  4{,}422 & +0.043   & 0.407 &  0.41    \\\\\nrf\\_moderate      & 15.756 & 0.056 &  4{,}940 & +0.021   & 0.492 &  0.34    \\\\\nrf\\_strict        & 15.810 & 0.061 &  2{,}496 & $-$0.034 & 0.607 & $-$0.25  \\\\\noracle\\_moderate  & 15.809 & 0.103 &  5{,}000 & $-$0.033 & 0.485 & $-$0.34  \\\\\noracle\\_strict    & 15.879 & 0.128 &  2{,}500 & $-$0.103 & 0.216 & $-$0.66  \\\\\nrandom\\_soft      & 16.062 & 0.079 &  4{,}422 & $-$0.286 & ${<}$0.001$^*$ & $-$2.77  \\\\\nrandom\\_moderate  & 15.998 & 0.090 &  4{,}940 & $-$0.222 & 0.028$^*$ & $-$1.50  \\\\\nrandom\\_strict    & 16.239 & 0.165 &  2{,}496 & $-$0.463 & 0.013$^*$ & $-$1.89  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pairwise comparisons (paired $t$-test, 5 seeds). $^*$\\,: $p<0.05$; $^{**}$\\,: $p<0.01$.}\n\\label{tab:curation_comparisons}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nComparison & $\\Delta$ PPL & $t$ & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nrf\\_strict vs random\\_strict     & 0.429 & $-$7.05 & 0.002$^{**}$ \\\\\nrf\\_moderate vs random\\_moderate & 0.242 & $-$3.77 & 0.020$^*$    \\\\\nrf\\_soft vs random\\_soft         & 0.328 & $-$17.98 & ${<}$0.001$^{**}$ \\\\\nrf\\_soft vs unfiltered           & 0.043 &    0.93 & 0.407        \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Reference Model Ablation}\n\\label{app:ref_ablation}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Reference model ablation. Classification accuracy (5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B generated text (nucleus, d0--d3) using different reference models for feature extraction.}\n\\label{tab:ref_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nReference Model & Architecture & Accuracy (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B (self) & GPT-NeoX & 78.6 $\\pm$ 0.6 \\\\\nOLMo-1B (cross) & OLMo & 74.5 $\\pm$ 0.7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Scale Full Results}\n\\label{app:cross_scale_full}\n\nThis section provides the full cross-scale analysis summarized in \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}.\nWe extend the benchmark to two 7--8B models: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, both under 4-bit NF4 QLoRA fine-tuning, generating depths $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\ldots, 5\\}$ with nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth).\nFeature extraction uses the same reference model (Pythia-1.4B) and 19-feature pipeline as all prior experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale comparison.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_scale} contrasts the two 7--8B models with the three 1--1.5B models on matched evaluation (nucleus sampling, depths 0--3).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-scale comparison: 4-class RF accuracy (\\%, depths 0--3, nucleus), $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70}$ (depths 0--5), and d0-vs-d1 AUC. CI: 95\\% bootstrap. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_scale}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & 4-class acc & $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70}$ & d0v1 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-2~XL (1.5B) & 83.3 & 4 & 0.999 \\\\\nOLMo-1B & 78.8 & 4 & 0.997 \\\\\nPythia-1.4B & 78.2 & 4 & 0.998 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLlama-3.1-8B & 70.2\\,[68.3, 72.4] & 4 & 0.949 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & 60.1\\,[58.0, 62.1] & ${>}5$ & 0.969 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{(1) Depth signal persists at 7--8B scale.}\nBoth 7--8B models substantially exceed the 25\\% chance baseline: Llama-3.1-8B achieves 70.2\\% (95\\% CI $[68.3, 72.4]$) and Qwen2.5-7B achieves 60.1\\% ($[58.0, 62.1]$), confirming that recursive depth remains estimable from perplexity-based features even when the generator is 5--7$\\times$ larger than the reference model.\nDepth-0 separability is preserved in both models: d0v1 AUC is 0.949 (Llama-3.1) and 0.969 (Qwen2.5).\n\n\\paragraph{(2) EDD at 7--8B scale.}\nAt threshold $T{=}0.70$, Qwen2.5-7B maintains all consecutive-pair AUCs above 0.70 through depth~5 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}), yielding $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70} > 5$---exceeding the $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ observed at 1--1.5B.\nLlama-3.1-8B matches the smaller models at $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$: its monotonic decay causes d4v5 (0.618) to drop below $T$, despite stronger signal at shallower depths.\nBoth larger generators produce weaker per-step signal than the 1--1.5B models (mean consecutive AUC: 0.786 for Llama-3.1, 0.782 for Qwen2.5, vs.\\ 0.852 at 1--1.5B).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Consecutive binary AUC for 7--8B models (nucleus $p{=}0.95$). Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic decay (d4v5 drops below $T{=}0.70$); Qwen2.5-7B shows a non-monotonic (U-shaped) pattern with all pairs above $T$.}\n\\label{tab:cross_scale_auc}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & d0v1 & d1v2 & d2v3 & d3v4 & d4v5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLlama-3.1-8B & 0.949 & 0.877 & 0.771 & 0.716 & 0.618 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & 0.969 & 0.729 & 0.709 & 0.732 & 0.772 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{(3) Divergent AUC trajectories and instruction-tuning effect.}\nAmong instruction-tuned models, Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic AUC decay consistent with the 1--1.5B pattern, while Qwen2.5-7B shows a U-shaped recovery beyond d2v3 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}).\nA base-model control (Llama-3.1-8B without instruction tuning) clarifies the causal direction: the base model achieves 59.9\\% four-class accuracy (95\\% CI [57.8, 62.15], $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$) with non-monotonic AUC decay (Table~\\ref{tab:llama3_base_auc}), while the instruction-tuned variant reaches 70.2\\% with strictly monotonic decay.\nThe base-model control experiment suggests instruction-tuning regularizes rather than confounds the depth signal.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pairwise AUC: Llama-3.1-8B Base vs.\\ Instruct.}\n\\label{tab:llama3_base_auc}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nVariant & $d_{0\\text{v}1}$ & $d_{1\\text{v}2}$ & $d_{2\\text{v}3}$ & $d_{3\\text{v}4}$ & $d_{4\\text{v}5}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase & 0.918 & 0.794 & 0.682 & 0.730 & 0.595 \\\\\nInstruct & 0.965 & 0.874 & 0.771 & 0.716 & 0.618 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe base model exhibits non-monotonic decay with a U-shaped recovery at $d_{3\\text{v}4}$, while the instruction-tuned variant shows strictly monotonic decay. Both variants exceed chance (AUC $> 0.5$) at all boundaries, confirming depth signal exists regardless of instruction-tuning, though instruction-tuning strengthens and regularizes it.\n\n\\paragraph{Caveats.}\nFor Qwen2.5-7B, the CI lower bound (58.0\\%) does not exclude sub-60\\% accuracy; Llama-3.1-8B's lower bound (68.3\\%) is more comfortably above chance but still below the 75--83\\% range at 1--1.5B.\nThe divergent AUC trajectories across two models caution against strong generalizations from only two 7--8B data points: both the monotonic (Llama-3.1) and non-monotonic (Qwen2.5) patterns are single observations, and further architectures are needed to establish which is the dominant regime at this scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Confounds.}\n\\label{app:cross_scale_confounds}\nFour confounds limit these results: (1)~4-bit NF4 QLoRA quantization (absent at 1--1.5B) may distort the perplexity distribution independently of depth; (2)~smaller sample size (500 vs.\\ 5{,}000 per depth) widens confidence intervals; (3)~evaluation is limited to nucleus sampling, leaving other strategies untested at this scale; (4)~vocabulary mismatch between 7--8B generators and the 1.4B reference model.\nThe base-model control partially addresses a former confound (instruction-tuning): since the base variant shows weaker signal with non-monotonic decay while the instruction-tuned variant shows stronger monotonic signal, instruction-tuning regularizes rather than confounds depth estimation.\nThe remaining confounds are not mutually independent---quantization and vocabulary mismatch both affect the perplexity distribution---making it infeasible to attribute the observed accuracy gap (60--70\\% vs.\\ 78--85\\% at 1--1.5B) to any single factor.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Boundary Generalization}\n\\label{app:cross_boundary}\n\nThe preceding experiments assume a single model family generates all recursive depths.\nIn practice, recursive contamination may traverse model boundaries: text generated by model~A is used to train model~B, whose output trains model~C.\nWe probe whether the depth signal persists under two relaxations of the single-model assumption.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model recursive chains.}\nWe construct a heterogeneous chain: human text (d0) $\\to$ Pythia-1.4B generates d1 $\\to$ OLMo-1B finetuned on Pythia-d1 generates d2 $\\to$ GPT-2~XL finetuned on OLMo-d2 generates d3.\nEach recursive step uses a \\emph{different} model family, eliminating the possibility that depth signal arises from a single model's idiosyncratic behavior.\nA 4-class Random Forest achieves 84.75\\% accuracy (95\\% CI $[81.0, 88.25]$), with per-depth F1 scores of 0.98 (d0), 0.88 (d1), 0.76 (d2), and 0.76 (d3).\nPairwise AUC remains high: d0-vs-d1 = 0.999 and d2-vs-d3 = 0.841.\nThese results suggest that distributional artifacts of recursive generation persist across model boundaries---providing preliminary evidence consistent with a process-level depth signal rather than individual generator artifacts.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound discussion.}\nThe heterogeneous chain confounds architecture identity with depth (d1=Pythia, d2=OLMo, d3=GPT-2~XL).\nHowever, if the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, near-perfect accuracy would be expected since architecture perfectly determines depth label.\nThe observed 84.75\\% with monotonically decreasing per-depth F1 (d0=0.98, d1=0.88, d2=0.76, d3=0.76) is more consistent with a depth-signal interpretation, as architecture fingerprinting would not produce this graded pattern.\nWe address this confound directly with a reverse-chain control below.\n\n\\paragraph{Reverse-chain control.}\nTo empirically test the architecture confound hypothesis, we constructed a reverse chain (GPT-2~XL $\\to$ OLMo $\\to$ Pythia) reversing the model-depth assignment.\nThe reverse chain achieves 85.75\\% (95\\% CI [82.0, 89.25]), statistically indistinguishable from the forward chain (84.75\\%, [81.0, 88.25]).\nIf the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, reversing the chain should invert depth predictions; the equivalent accuracy under both orderings provides empirical evidence against the architecture confound.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-domain transfer.}\nAll prior experiments use WikiText-103 as the seed corpus.\nTo test domain dependence, we repeat the pipeline with arXiv abstracts as the seed text (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth, d0--d3).\nFive-fold cross-validation yields 74.1\\% $\\pm$ 2.0\\% accuracy, compared to ${\\sim}$78\\% on the WikiText seed.\nPer-depth F1 shows the familiar pattern: d0 = 1.00, d1 = 0.78, d2 = 0.52, d3 = 0.66---with d2 remaining the most challenging class.\nThe 4pp accuracy gap relative to WikiText likely reflects the narrower stylistic range of scientific abstracts, which may reduce the distributional contrast between adjacent depths.\nThese results provide preliminary evidence that the perplexity-based features capture properties of the recursive generation process rather than domain-specific artifacts of the seed corpus.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Seed Replication}\n\\label{app:seed_replication}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:seed_replication} reports within-seed and cross-seed classification accuracy for Pythia-1.4B (nucleus, depths 0--3) under two independent LoRA training seeds.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-seed transfer (Pythia-1.4B nucleus). Baseline seed=42 is the primary benchmark LoRA; seed=43 is an independent replication. $\\Delta$: difference from respective comparison point.}\n\\label{tab:seed_replication}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Acc (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nWithin-seed (seed=42) & 78.20 & --- \\\\\nWithin-seed (seed=43) & 77.15 & $-$1.05$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCross-seed (train 42$\\to$test 43) & 77.85 & \\\\\nCross-seed (train 43$\\to$test 42) & 75.68 & \\\\\nCross-seed avg & 76.76 & $-$1.30$^\\ddagger$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$vs.\\ baseline (78.20\\%). $^\\ddagger$vs.\\ within-seed avg (78.07\\%).}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe seed=43 LoRA achieves 77.15\\% (95\\% CI [76.4, 78.15]), within 1.05\\,pp of the seed=42 baseline.\nCross-seed transfer averages 76.76\\%, a 1.30\\,pp deficit relative to within-seed performance.\nTop-5 feature importance is identical across seeds (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}, \\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_ppl}, \\texttt{var\\_ppl}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}), confirming that depth signal arises from the recursive process itself rather than idiosyncratic LoRA weight configurations.\n\n\\subsection{Generation Length Ablation}\n\\label{app:length_ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:length_ablation} reports classification accuracy as a function of passage length (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, depths 0--3).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Generation length ablation (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, 5-fold CV). Accuracy increases monotonically with passage length; the 9.47\\,pp spread confirms that longer passages provide stronger statistical signal.}\n\\label{tab:length_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\nPassage length (tokens) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\n128 & 68.35 \\\\\n256 & 73.80 \\\\\n512 & 77.82 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDepth signal is detectable from 128 tokens (2.7$\\times$ chance) and saturates near 500 tokens.\nThe monotonic increase reflects the statistical nature of the features: longer passages yield more stable estimates of perplexity percentiles and surprisal variance, reducing within-class overlap at adjacent depth boundaries.\n\n\\subsection{Mixed-Depth Corpus}\n\\label{app:mixed_depth}\n\nPer-passage classification is largely independent of corpus composition: evaluating on a corpus containing mixed proportions of d0--d3 passages (rather than balanced classes) yields macro-recall $\\Delta{=}{-}1.62$\\,pp relative to the balanced benchmark (within the 2\\,pp tolerance).\nIndividual class deltas range from $-$0.45\\,pp to $-$2.43\\,pp (d1 shows the largest shift), confirming that the RF classifier operates on per-sample features without sensitivity to the class distribution of the evaluation corpus.\n\n\\subsection{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended_discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}\nCurrent data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.\nRecursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.\nThe 70--85\\% in-distribution accuracy and $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest depth-graduated filtering is feasible within known model--strategy configurations, though the d1/d2 boundary inversion under cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) means reliable curation requires either known decoding strategy metadata or an upstream strategy-identification step; a pilot study (Appendix~\\ref{app:curation_poc}) yields an inconclusive RF-vs-unfiltered comparison ($p>0.4$), though RF-filtered subsets significantly outperform size-matched random subsampling ($p<0.001$--$0.02$); large-scale validation remains open.\nImportance weighting \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.\n\n\\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}\nDepth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.\nWatermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.\nSource attribution \\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.\nDepth estimation characterizes \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.\nA layered provenance system combining these three signals---watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth---could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.\n",

Here are the key findings about ACL ARR reviewer guidelines, review form criteria, and scoring for 2025-2026:

## Reviewer Score Components

Starting with February 2025, ARR reviewers are asked to provide three scores: soundness, excitement, and overall recommendation. The soundness scores must be justified by the text of the review.

## Overall Recommendation Scoring Scale

The overall recommendation scale includes: 2 = Resubmit next cycle (substantial revisions that can be completed by the next ARR cycle), 1.5 = Resubmit after next cycle (substantial revisions that cannot be completed by the next ARR cycle), and 1 = Do not resubmit (paper has to be fully redone, or is not relevant to the *ACL community).

## Additional Evaluation Criteria

The review form includes assessment of:
- Whether authors have adequately discussed limitations and potential positive and negative societal impacts, with constructive suggestions for improvement if not.
- Ethical issues with the paper and the extent to which they have been acknowledged or addressed by the authors.
- Whether there is enough information in the paper for a reader to reproduce the main results or build upon the work.

## Area Chair Meta-review Scoring

The meta-review score option for "Consider for Award" (5) applies to papers that could be considered for an outstanding paper award at an *ACL conference (up to top 2.5% papers).

## Key Review Standards

ARR reviewers are instructed to write a review as if the paper were being considered for the ACL conference, so low scores may indicate the submission is not a good fit for ACL, even if it may be a good fit for other venues.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

# ACL Rolling Review: Scoring Criteria

## 1. **Reviewer Confidence**
Scale: 1-5
- **5**: "Positive that my evaluation is correct. I read the paper very carefully and am familiar with related work."
- **4**: Quite confident; unlikely to have missed important details
- **3**: Reasonably sure, but may have missed specifics
- **2**: Willing to defend evaluation, but likely missed some details
- **1**: Outside expertise area or paper is difficult to understand

## 2. **Soundness**
Scale: 1-5 (with 0.5 increments)
- **5**: "one of the most thorough I have seen, given its type"
- **4**: Sufficient support for claims; minor experiments could enhance
- **3**: Main claims adequately supported; minor points need details
- **2**: Main claims lack sufficient support; major technical problems
- **1**: Insufficient depth or not relevant to ACL

## 3. **Excitement**
Scale: 1-5 (with 0.5 increments)
- **5**: "Highly Exciting"; would recommend widely
- **4**: "Exciting"; would mention to others
- **3**: "Interesting"; might mention or attend presentation
- **2**: "Potentially Interesting"; resonates with some community members
- **1**: Does not resonate; unrelated to computational linguistics

## 4. **Overall Assessment**
Scale: 1-5 (with 0.5 increments)
- **5**: Consider for Award (top 2.5%)
- **4**: Conference acceptance recommended
- **3**: Findings acceptance recommended
- **2**: Needs substantial revisions
- **1**: Reject; requires complete rework

## 5. **Reproducibility**
Scale: 1-5
- **5**: Results easily reproducible
- **4**: Mostly reproducible with minor variations
- **3**: Reproducible with difficulty; underspecified parameters
- **2**: Reproduction challenging; unavailable data
- **1**: Cannot reproduce results

## 6. **Datasets & Software**
Scale: 1-5 each
- **5**: Enabling for future research
- **4**: Useful for ongoing work
- **3**: Potentially useful
- **2**: Documentary value only
- **1**: Not available

## 7. **Author Identity Knowledge**
Scale: 1-5
- **5**: Identity known from double-blind violation
- **4**: Known from preprint/workshop
- **3**: Identifiable from submission content
- **2**: Identified via external communication
- **1**: No educated guess possible

Breaking the Curse of Recursion by Accumulating Real and Synthetic Data | OpenReview","url":"https://openreview.net/forum?id=5B2K4LRgmz"},{"title":"Escaping Model Collapse via Synthetic Data Verification: Near-term Improvements and Long-term ConvergenceThis project is supported by the AI2050 program at Schmidt Sciences (Grant G-24-66104) and Army Research Office Award W911NF-23-1-0030. We also thank Cong Ma from UChicago, Hongning Wang and Bo Li from Tsinghua, Pinyan Lu and Gavin Tang from Shanghai University of Finance and Economics for helpful discussions and suggestions.","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2510.16657v1"}]

Based on the search results, I found relevant research by Rylan Schaeffer and collaborators on model collapse and synthetic data:

## Key Findings

While previous studies largely assumed that new data replace old data over time, research by Gerstgrasser, Schaeffer and colleagues demonstrates that accumulating successive generations of synthetic data alongside the original real data avoids model collapse.

The research addresses your query directly: Replacing data leads to collapse, but accumulating data avoids collapse, with test loss upper bounded by a small constant when data accumulate.

In a more recent 2025 position paper, Schaeffer and colleagues note that many prominent model collapse papers assume data are entirely deleted after each model-fitting iteration and that subsequent models are trained entirely on synthetic data, which is a key distinction from more realistic scenarios.

According to Schaeffer et al. (2025) and Kazdan et al. (2024), collapse progresses at such a slow pace that it is rarely a practical concern in real-world applications.

The research suggests that model collapse is **not inevitable** when data management strategies like accumulation are employed, contrary to earlier warnings about recursive training on synthetic data alone.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

      "Based on the search results, I found relevant research by Rylan Schaeffer and collaborators on model collapse and synthetic data:\n\n## Key Findings\n\nWhile previous studies largely assumed that new data replace old data over time, research by Gerstgrasser, Schaeffer and colleagues demonstrates that accumulating successive generations of synthetic data alongside the original real data avoids model collapse.\n\nThe research addresses your query directly: Replacing data leads to collapse, but accumulating data avoids collapse, with test loss upper bounded by a small constant when data accumulate.\n\nIn a more recent 2025 position paper, Schaeffer and colleagues note that many prominent model collapse papers assume data are entirely deleted after each model-fitting iteration and that subsequent models are trained entirely on synthetic data, which is a key distinction from more realistic scenarios.\n\nAccording to Schaeffer et al. (2025) and Kazdan et al. (2024), collapse progresses at such a slow pace that it is rarely a practical concern in real-world applications.\n\nThe research suggests that model collapse is **not inevitable** when data management strategies like accumulation are employed, contrary to earlier warnings about recursive training on synthetic data alone."

I found the paper you're looking for. Here's what the search returned:

**Title:** "Curated Synthetic Data Doesn't Have to Collapse: A Theoretical Study of Generative Retraining with Pluralistic Preferences"

**Authors:** Ali Falahati, Mohammad Mohammadi Amiri, Kate Larson, and Lukasz Golab

**Key Findings:**

The paper addresses a critical challenge: when synthetic outputs are curated based on a fixed reward signal, the model tends to collapse onto a narrow set of outputs that over-optimize that objective. However, the authors show that collapse can be mitigated through curation based on multiple reward functions, and they prove that under certain conditions, the model converges to a stable distribution that allocates probability mass across competing high-reward regions.

The limiting distribution preserves diversity and provably satisfies a weighted Nash bargaining solution, offering a formal interpretation of value aggregation in synthetic retraining loops.

**Publication Date:** May 8, 2026

The paper directly tackles the problem of mode collapse in recursive generative model retraining by considering pluralistic preferences from multiple reward functions rather than relying on a single objective.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

A Statistical Analysis of Language Model Collapse","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2404.05090v1"}]

I found the paper! Here are the key details about arXiv 2509.04796:

## Paper Overview

The paper is titled "Knowledge Collapse in LLMs: When Fluency Survives but Facts Fail under Recursive Synthetic Training" by Figarri Keisha and 4 other authors, submitted on September 5, 2025.

## Key Concept

Large language models increasingly rely on synthetic data due to human-written content scarcity, yet recursive training on model-generated outputs leads to model collapse, a degenerative process threatening factual reliability.

Knowledge collapse is defined as a distinct three-stage phenomenon where factual accuracy deteriorates while surface fluency persists, creating "confidently wrong" outputs that pose critical risks in accuracy-dependent domains.

## Main Contributions

The paper makes four key contributions:

1. Defining knowledge collapse as a three-stage process: Stage A (Knowledge Preservation), Stage B (Knowledge Collapse, i.e., the "confidently wrong" transition), and Stage C (Instruction-following Collapse).

2. Demonstrating conditional degradation, showing that collapse trajectory depends on prompt format, unlike traditional prompt-agnostic collapse.

3. Proposing mitigation via domain-specific training, delaying accuracy decay by 15× compared to general synthetic training.

4. Providing an open-source framework for reproducible evaluation of epistemic degradation across models.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

I found the paper! Here's what I discovered:

## "Position: Model Collapse Does Not Mean What You Think"

The paper is authored by Rylan Schaeffer, Joshua Kazdan, Alvan Caleb Arulandu, and Sanmi Koyejo, and was submitted to arXiv on March 5, 2025 (arXiv:2503.03150).

### Main Arguments:

The paper addresses concerns about model collapse—a degradation in future generative models' performance when trained on synthetic data—noting that industry leaders and premier research journals have prophesied catastrophic societal consequences, while the position piece contends this widespread narrative fundamentally misunderstands the scientific evidence.

### Key Findings:

The research highlights that model collapse research actually encompasses eight distinct and at times conflicting definitions of model collapse, and argues that inconsistent terminology within and between papers has hindered building a comprehensive understanding.

The authors conclude that certain predicted claims of model collapse rely on assumptions and conditions that poorly match real-world conditions, and several prominent collapse scenarios are readily avoidable.

Overall, the position paper argues that model collapse has been warped from a nuanced multifaceted consideration into an oversimplified threat, and that the evidence suggests specific harms more likely under society's current trajectory have received disproportionately less attention.

The paper is available on arXiv at https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03150


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

I've found detailed information about the Wenzek 2020 CCNet paper. Here's what I found:

## Overview

CCNet: Extracting High Quality Monolingual Datasets from Web Crawl Data is a paper by Wenzek et al. published in the Proceedings of the Twelfth Language Resources and Evaluation Conference in 2020.

## Purpose and Approach

The paper describes an automatic pipeline to extract massive high-quality monolingual datasets from Common Crawl for a variety of languages, following data processing from fastText that deduplicates documents and identifies their language, augmented with a filtering step to select documents that are close to high quality corpora like Wikipedia.

## Perplexity-Based Filtering

The filtering is achieved by training a language model on the target source and using perplexity as the document's scoring function. More specifically, CCNet uses a 5-gram Kneser-Ney language model implemented in the KenLM library trained on the target domain.

## Pipeline Steps

The pipeline processes every document of the WET files independently: deduplicating paragraphs, performing language identification and computing language model perplexity score, then regrouping documents into JSON files by language and perplexity score.

The paper's official implementation and tools are available on GitHub at the Facebook Research ccnet repository for downloading and processing Common Crawl data.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

em-dash，建议全部改为逗号/句号拆分。\n- **-ing phrase ending**: abstract.tex line 6 \"finding EDD...\" 作为分词短语结尾是 AI 写作标志。\n- **DivEye year**: bib 标注 2026 但 arXiv 为 2025-09，若 TMLR 已于 2025 发表应修正 year。\n- empirical thoroughness 上做得出色，是一个 well-engineered 实证研究。主要弱点是：(1) 核心方法论贡献有限（标准特征+标准分类器，核心发现可从已有理论预测）；(2) 叙事 framing 存在多处 overclaim 且未回应直接挑战前提的文献；(3) Abstract 可读性问题。对于 Findings track，如果 N1-N3 得到修复（明确增量贡献定位、回应 Schaeffer 2025、修复 Abstract），论文质量足够。当前版本在新颖性 positioning 上仍有 gap，可能触发 excitement 降分。\n\n建议打分：**Overall 3.5**（介于 Findings acceptance 和 needs revision 之间，偏向 accept if N1-N3 ## Reviewer 1 审稿报告

**Overall**: 3.0 (Resubmit next cycle)
**一句话总结**: 核心方法新颖且实验覆盖面宽，但主表格缺统计不确定性度量、EDD 跨模型统计支撑不均、以及 accumulation/quality-control 实验缺失等问题需在 camera-ready 前解决。

### Must-Fix

**N1** [critical] Abstract 数值精度问题
- Abstract 写 "70--85% four-class accuracy"，Table 2 实际为 69.6%--85.4%。下界 69.6% 向上取整为 70%。
- 建议改为 "approximately 70--85%" 或 "69.6--85.4%"。

**N2** [critical] Curation PoC 与 narrative 不一致
- Discussion 将 depth-graduated curation 作为核心 motivation，但 PoC 中 RF-filtered vs unfiltered 无统计显著差异（p=0.407）。
- 建议主文降低 curation 强调或明确标注 inconclusive。

### Should-Fix

**W1** EDD 在 GPT-2 XL 上的 boundary case（AUC=0.750 恰等于 T=0.75, p=0.505）
**W2** Cross-strategy transfer failure 与 "universal fingerprint" narrative 的张力
**W3** 95% CI 未报告在 primary result tables（Table 2, Table 5）

### 历史 Issues 验证
- ✅ R1-W2 (d0 inflation): RESOLVED
- ✅ R5-W1 (Single LoRA): RESOLVED
- ⚠️ R1-N2 (Tables 缺 CI): PARTIALLY — 附录有但主表格无
- ⚠️ R4-N1 (EDD 统计限定): PARTIALLY — 限定到 Pythia 但 "graded statistical support" 仍 euphemistic
- ⚠️ R5-N2 (Reference model 循环): PARTIALLY — discussed in Limitations but no new ablation
- ⚠️ R5-W5 (EDD classifier-dependence): PARTIALLY — convergence shown but not explicitly stated
- ❌ R5-W4 (Missing references): NOT ADDRESSED — McCullagh, Rennie&Srebro, CCNet, Schaeffer 均未引
- ❌ R1-W3 (LoRA rank sensitivity): NOT ADDRESSED
- ❌ R7-W3 (Partial mixing): NOT ADDRESSED
- ❌ R7-W4 (Depth vs quality confound): NOT ADDRESSED

### 主要优势
1. 问题新颖性
2. 系统性实验设计
3. 诚实的 Limitations
4. 特征可解释性
5. Ablation 充分性

---

请对以上每个 Must-Fix 和 Should-Fix 逐条给出 [AGREE] 或 [REBUT] 回应。特别关注你是否同意 R1 对 Abstract 取整问题、curation PoC、和 accumulation 场景的判断。

{
  "text": "## 交叉回应 — Reviewer 2 对 Reviewer 1 审稿意见的逐条回应\n\n---\n\n### Must-Fix 回应\n\n**[AGREE] R1-N1: Abstract 数值精度问题**\n\n同意这是需要修复的问题。Table 2 (atlas.tex line 24-31) 明确显示最低值为 Pythia top-k 69.6%，将其取整为 \"70%\" 构成向上偏误。虽然差异仅 0.4pp，但在学术论文中 Abstract 数字必须可追溯。\"approximately 70--85%\" 或直接写 \"69.6--85.4%\" 均可接受。我倾向后者，因为 Abstract 已经数字密集，再加一个模糊限定词 (\"approximately\") 反而增加阅读负担。\n\n**[AGREE] R1-N2: Curation PoC 与 narrative 不一致**\n\n强烈同意。这与我的 W2 判断一致。Discussion.tex line 7 使用 \"enables depth-graduated data curation\" 是 overclaim。具体证据：\n- Table 14 (section_appendix.tex): rf_soft vs unfiltered: Δ=+0.043 PPL, p=0.407, d_z=0.41\n- rf_moderate vs unfiltered: p=0.492\n- rf_strict vs unfiltered: 方向甚至反转 (PPL +0.034 worse)\n\n唯一 significant 的正面结论是 RF-filtered vs size-matched random subsampling (p<0.001--0.02, Table 15)，这证明 RF 选择的数据比随机子集更好，但未证明 depth-filtered > unfiltered full corpus。Discussion 主文必须区分这两个不同的 claim。\n\n---\n\n### Should-Fix 回应\n\n**[AGREE] R1-W1: EDD 在 GPT-2 XL 上的 boundary case**\n\n同意。atlas.tex line 122-123 明确报告 GPT-2 XL d3-vs-d4 AUC = 0.750，p = 0.505。AUC 恰好等于阈值 T=0.75 且统计不显著，这使得 GPT-2 XL 的 EDD₀.₇₅=4 判定在技术上是边界情况。论文应至少在 Table 4 (EDD sensitivity) 或文中加注 \"GPT-2 XL reaches the threshold marginally (AUC=0.750, p=0.505)\" 以避免读者误读为三个模型均有强证据支撑 EDD=4。\n\n补充我的独立判断：论文在 atlas.tex line 123-124 已部分处理此问题（\"establishing a consistent depth-4 decay boundary with graded confidence rather than uniform statistical significance\"），但这一限定出现在正文深处，Abstract 和 Contribution bullet 未体现。\n\n**[AGREE] R1-W2: Cross-strategy transfer failure 与 \"universal fingerprint\" 的张力**\n\n强烈同意。这与我的 W3 一致。具体矛盾证据：\n- Introduction line 14: \"universal and scale-variant\"\n- generalization.tex line 110: \"signal universality\"\n- 但 cross-strategy mean accuracy = 59.1% (Table 6)，d1-d3 balanced accuracy = 45.9% (仅 1.4× chance)\n- Temperature holdout d1v2 AUC 甚至 inverted (< 0.5)\n\n在 cross-strategy transfer 失败且需要 strategy-conditioned classifiers 的情况下，\"universal\" 用词不成立。Signal 是 strategy-dependent，不是 universal。\n\n**[REBUT] R1-W3: 95% CI 未报告在 primary result tables**\n\n部分反驳。我理解 CI 增加可信度的诉求，但需要权衡：\n1. Table 2 (主矩阵) 报告的是 5-fold CV accuracy。对于 n=5000/depth × 4 depths = 20000 samples per cell 的 5-fold CV，点估计的 standard error 极小（典型 ±0.5-0.8pp），加 CI 会使表格拥挤而信息增量有限。\n2. 论文在 cross-scale 结果 (Table 8, section_appendix.tex line 335-339) 确实报告了 bootstrap 95% CI [68.3, 72.4] / [58.0, 62.1]，这些是 n=500/depth 的小样本情况，CI 更有信息价值。\n3. 折中方案：在 Table 2 caption 加一句 \"standard errors across folds are ≤0.8pp for all cells\" 即可，无需在每个 cell 塞 CI 区间。\n\n因此：同意 cross-scale 小样本结果需要 CI（已有），不同意主表格必须逐 cell 加 CI。\n\n---\n\n### 历史 Issues 验证回应\n\n**[AGREE] R1: R5-W4 NOT ADDRESSED (Missing references)**\n完全同意。我的审稿报告独立得出相同结论——Schaeffer et al. 2025、CCNet (Wenzek et al. 2020)、McCullagh 1980 均未引用。这是我的 N1 (critical) 级别问题。\n\n**[AGREE] R1: R1-W3 (LoRA rank sensitivity) NOT ADDRESSED**\n同意。论文使用单一 LoRA 配置 (r=16, α=32)。Appendix seed replication (Table 12) 验证了 cross-seed stability 但未验证 rank sensitivity。如果 r=4 或 r=64 产生不同 depth signal，这将质疑 signal 的 process-level nature。不过我认为这更接近 Should-Fix 而非 Must-Fix：cross-seed replication (Δ=-1.05pp) 和 classifier convergence (Δ≤0.4pp) 共同提供了间接证据，表明 signal 不太可能是 LoRA-specific artifact。\n\n**[REBUT] R1: R7-W3 (Partial mixing) NOT ADDRESSED — 严重程度判断不同**\n我认为 partial mixing（真实场景中 training corpus 包含混合比例的各 depth 数据）确实是一个 open question，但论文 Limitations 已明确讨论 (\"wild recursive contamination, where models are pretrained on mixed corpora containing prior-generation outputs at unknown proportions\"，conclusion.tex line 14-15)。对于首篇 formalize 该 task 的论文，要求同时解决 clean lab setting 和 partial mixing scenario 可能过高。这是 future work 而非 fatal flaw。\n\n**[AGREE] R1: R7-W4 (Depth vs quality confound) NOT ADDRESSED**\n同意这是一个值得关注的问题。LoRA fine-tuning 本身可能在各 depth 引入质量差异（不同 depth 的 training loss 不同，暗示 memorization 程度不同——appendix §multi_tau 实际展示了这一点）。如果 classifier 部分地利用了 quality-as-proxy 而非纯粹的 depth signal，这会影响对 method 本质的理解。不过 cross-model chain 实验 (84.75%) 提供了部分反驳——如果 signal 纯粹是单模型 LoRA quality artifact，跨模型链不应保持如此高精度。\n\n**[AGREE] R1: R4-N1 \"graded statistical support\" 仍 euphemistic**\n同意。\"Graded statistical support\" 是一个自创的委婉说法，掩盖了 OLMo p=0.091 (marginal) 和 GPT-2 XL p=0.505 (non-significant) 的实际情况。审稿人期望看到的是直接报告：\"significant for Pythia (p=0.001), marginal for OLMo (p=0.091), non-significant for GPT-2 XL (p=0.505)\"。Abstract 只写 Pythia 的 p=0.001 而用 \"graded statistical support\" 概括其余两个是选择性强调。\n\n---\n\n### 综合判断\n\nR1 的 Overall 3.0 (Resubmit next cycle) 比我的 3.5 略低。我认为差异合理：R1 额外关注了 LoRA rank sensitivity 和 partial mixing 这两个我未重点审查的维度。但我认为对于 Findings track（非 main conference），当前版本经修复 Abstract + curation narrative + novelty positioning 后可接受，不需要 resubmit next cycle 级别的大修。R1 的 accumulation 实验缺失判断合理但更适合作为 future work limitation 而非 blocking issue。\n\n我维持 Overall 3.5，条件是 N1-N3 (我的 Must-Fix) + R1-N1/N2 得到修复。",

  "project": "recursive_gen_depth_est"
}

_source line 89 · 2026-05-17T08:46:45.299Z_

[Round 1 审稿请求]:
## Review Assignment: R2 — Novelty & Related Work

**Venue:** EMNLP 2026 Main (via ACL Rolling Review)
**Paper:** "Ordinal Depth Estimation for Recursive Language Model Generations: A Depth Degradation Atlas Across Scales and Strategies"

**Scoring system (ARR 2025+):** Three scores on 1–5 scale: Soundness, Excitement, Overall Assessment.
- 5 = Top ~2%, exceptional
- 4 = Strong, warrants acceptance at a top *ACL venue
- 3 = Good, could be published but may not be top-tier
- 2 = Borderline, has merit but significant flaws
- 1 = Poor, significant flaws

**Your focus:** Novelty, significance, related work positioning, writing quality, community fit.

### Paper Summary
The paper formalizes "ordinal generation depth estimation" as a new task: predicting how many recursive generation cycles (d ∈ {0,1,2,3}) a text passage has undergone. Key contributions:
1. Task formalization + benchmark (3 models × 3 strategies × 4 depths)
2. "Depth Degradation Atlas" — systematic empirical map of how text statistical properties evolve (feature universality, monotonic AUC decay, strategy-dependent trajectory reversal)
3. Two-stage pipeline for cross-strategy deployment (75.2% mean accuracy)
Plus: 14B scale validation, prompted generation ecological validity, wild-data calibration

### Evaluate These Dimensions

**A. Novelty Assessment**
- Is ordinal depth estimation genuinely new? Any prior work the authors missed?
- Is the Depth Degradation Atlas a novel contribution type, or is it "just" a thorough empirical study?
- Is the two-stage pipeline novel or obvious engineering?
- How novel are the findings (feature universality, trajectory reversal)?

**B. Related Work (Section 2)**
- Coverage: Are all relevant areas covered?
  - Model collapse: Shumailov et al. 2024, Dohmatob et al. 2024, Gerstgrasser et al. 2024, Alemohammad et al. 2024, Drayson et al. 2025
  - Detection: DetectGPT, Fast-DetectGPT, Binoculars, LOKI, DivEye
  - Decoding: Holtzman et al. 2020, Garcés Arias et al. 2025
  - Attribution: Uchendu et al. 2020, Li et al. 2024b
  - Data curation: DSIR, QuRating
  - Watermarking: Kirchenbauer et al. 2023
- Missing references? Consider: RAID benchmark, PHD, multi-class detection methods, training data attribution, more recent 2025 model collapse works
- Is the positioning against binary detectors convincing?
- Does the Related Work clearly identify the gap this paper fills?

**C. Significance & Impact**
- How important is depth estimation for the NLP community?
- Is the practical application (data curation) convincing?
- Does the 15-20% FPR on web data limit practical deployment?
- Is the temporal signal (pre/post 2023) compelling evidence?

**D. Writing Quality**
- Abstract: Does it effectively convey all four elements (problem, importance, method, results)?
- Introduction: Is the narrative compelling? Does it follow problem→gap→insight→method→results?
- Are contributions clearly stated and supported?
- Section 6 "Atlas Visualizations" — is this section adequate (only one sentence before figures)?
- Figures and tables: information density, clarity, captions?
- Any AI writing artifacts (dashes, -ing phrases, excessive transition words)?

**E. Community Fit (EMNLP)**
- Does this align with EMNLP's emphasis on empirical methods?
- Is the empirical methodology representative of EMNLP-quality work?
- Would the community find the Atlas findings useful?

**F. Citation Adequacy**
- Count and quality of references (currently ~22 references)
- Are all baselines, benchmarks, and foundational works cited?
- Any potential missing citations that a reviewer would flag?

Provide: (1) numbered strengths, (2) numbered weaknesses, (3) questions for authors, (4) preliminary scores (Soundness, Excitement, Overall) with justification.

_source line 96 · 2026-05-17T08:47:02.134Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_015QHVRF2xCWGC7rLuooTX2h`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	As large language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\to$data$\to$model loop arises, yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.
3	We formalize \emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K passages spanning 3 model families, 3 decoding strategies, and 4 generation depths.
4	A Random Forest over 19 perplexity-based features achieves 70--85\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\%), with perplexity tail statistics emerging as consistent depth markers; a parsimonious subset of 5 features retains 97.6\% of full performance.
5	We define the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, finding $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for the best-characterized family, and show that cross-model transfer reaches 66.4\% (2.7$\times$ chance).
6	Systematic generalization across seven axes (including cross-scale with 7B models at 70.2\%, cross-domain, and LoRA-rank ablation) confirms that depth signal is robust and resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text (zero-shot LLMs: 30--36\%, 42\,pp below RF).
7	\end{abstract}
8	
```

</details>

### Tool result

_source line 98 · 2026-05-17T08:47:02.584Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01G2k2HBRmsebNtZKNQb9Xtz`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:intro}
3	
4	The text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.
5	As synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.
6	This model$\to$data$\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical; it is already occurring in the wild. Yet we lack the ability to determine how many recursive iterations a given text has undergone, a distinction that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.
7	
8	Existing tools address adjacent but distinct problems.
9	Binary detectors such as Binoculars \cite{binoculars2024}, Fast-DetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, and DivEye \cite{diveye2026} classify text as human-written or machine-generated, collapsing all depths $d \geq 1$ into a single category.
10	Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} requires proactive embedding and cannot be applied retroactively.
11	The model collapse literature \cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} assumes depth is known a priori; \citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} filter synthetic data via binary detection without distinguishing depths.
12	No existing method estimates the ordinal recursive generation depth of a text passage from its content alone.
13	
14	We find that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints are \textbf{consistent in direction but scale-dependent in magnitude} across the three model families and three decoding strategies studied.
15	Specifically, perplexity tail statistics (the 90th-percentile perplexity, \textsc{p90\_ppl}, and surprisal variance, \textsc{var\_surprisal}) emerge as dominant depth indicators, ranking in the top two features by importance in 9/9 and 8/9 model--strategy configurations, respectively.
16	Instruction-tuned variants exhibit monotonically decreasing AUC (regularization), whereas base models can show non-monotonic trajectories, suggesting that instruction tuning acts as a regularizer on the depth signal rather than introducing degradation.
17	
18	We formalize recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and introduce a lightweight pipeline: compute 19 reference-model perplexity features (effective dimensionality ${\approx}$4--5 due to multicollinearity; \S\ref{sec:atlas:features}) and classify with a Random Forest into $K{+}1$ depth classes.
19	To characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \times 3$ grid of model families $\times$ decoding strategies spanning 4 depths (180K samples).
20	The atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories and we introduce the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.
21	
22	Our contributions are:
23	\begin{itemize}
24	    \item \textbf{Task formalization and benchmark.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification, introduce the EDD metric, and construct a 180K-sample benchmark spanning 3 model families $\times$ 3 decoding strategies $\times$ 4 depths.
25	
26	    \item \textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} A systematic $3 \times 3$ characterization reveals that perplexity tail statistics dominate depth discrimination (top-2 in 9/9 and 8/9 cells), decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories qualitatively, and $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for the best-characterized family (Pythia, $p{=}0.001$; consistent across all three families).
27	
28	    \item \textbf{Generalization across seven axes.} Cross-model recursive chains (84.75\%), cross-scale observations on two 7B models (70.2\% and 60.1\%), cross-domain transfer (arXiv 74.1\%), temperature robustness ($\tau \in [0.5, 0.9]$), and reference model robustness (67--79\% accuracy across three reference models). A surface-text ablation (zero-shot LLMs at 30--36\%, 42\,pp below RF) confirms the signal resides in distributional statistics, while requiring only 19 features and a Random Forest for GPU-free inference.
29	\end{itemize}
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Model Collapse Theory.}
5	\citet{shumailov2024_nature} demonstrate that recursive training on model outputs erodes distributional tails; \citet{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} formalize this via scaling laws \cite{seddik2024synthetic}, \citet{dohmatob2025_strong_collapse} establish strong collapse guarantees, and the MAD framework \cite{alemohammad2024_mad} extends the analysis to self-consuming models.
6	Subsequent work addresses mitigation \cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate,drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse,yi2026_escaping_collapse,falahati2026_curated,wenzek2020_ccnet}, non-monotonic dynamics \cite{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement}, convergence \cite{marchi2024_heat_death,wang2026_contamination,knowledge_collapse2025,dohmatob2024_demystified}, and diversity decline \cite{guo2024curious,briesch2024selfconsuming,padmakumar2024diversity,sigma2026_spectral,synthetic_eggs2026}.
7	\citet{schaeffer2025} argue that ``model collapse'' conflates multiple distinct phenomena, underscoring the need for operationally precise metrics.
8	Dohmatob et al.\ predict that recursive training erodes distributional tails as a theoretical scaling law \citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; we operationalize this prediction into a deployable ordinal classifier, revealing strategy-dependent trajectories and non-monotonic 7B behavior that the theory does not predict.
9	All assume depth is known a priori; we address the inverse problem of estimating depth from text alone.
10	
11	\paragraph{AI Text Detection.}
12	The detection literature \cite{wu2025_detection_survey} spans statistical methods (DetectGPT \cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt}, Fast-DetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, Binoculars \cite{binoculars2024}, DivEye \cite{diveye2026}, GLTR \cite{gehrmann2019_gltr}, Ghostbuster \cite{verma2024_ghostbuster}, DNA-GPT \cite{yang2024_dnagpt}) and watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark}, with known limits from paraphrasing \cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase}, distribution shift \cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and generator barriers \cite{pu2026_generator_barrier}.
13	M4-SemEval \cite{wang2024_m4_semeval} benchmarks multilingual detection.
14	All these methods perform binary classification, discarding ordinal depth information; our approach outputs ordinal depth labels, complementing rather than replacing binary detection.
15	
16	\paragraph{Adjacent Tasks.}
17	EditLens \cite{editlens2026} estimates editing extent but does not model recursive chains; \citet{uchendu2020_attribution} classify \emph{which} model generated text; LOKI \cite{loki2025} benchmarks synthetic data detection across modalities.
18	\citet{mohamed2025_broken_telephone} study iterative generation as a ``broken telephone'' effect, characterizing \emph{how} text degrades without classifying which iteration a passage comes from.
19	Our task performs the inverse, ordinal depth estimation from text alone, discriminating between adjacent depths rather than merely detecting aggregate degradation.
20	
21	\paragraph{Ordinal Classification.}
22	Ordinal classification has a long history \cite{mccullagh1980_ordinal}: \citet{frank2001} reduce ordinal problems to nested binary tasks, and \citet{rennie2005} introduce loss functions tailored to discrete ordered labels.
23	CORAL \cite{coral2020} enforces rank consistency via ordered binary tasks; CLOC \cite{cloc2025} extends contrastive learning with multi-margin ordinal losses.
24	We adopt both as baselines (\S\ref{sec:generalization}) to confirm the signal is accessible to principled ordinal methods.
25	
26	\paragraph{Decoding Strategy Effects.}
27	Decoding strategies shape text statistics in ways that interact with depth signals \cite{holtzman2020_nucleus,finlayson2024_closing,garces_arias2025_decoding_decoded,wiher2022_decoding,basu2021_mirostat}.
28	Our Depth Degradation Atlas (\S\ref{sec:atlas}) reveals that these strategies induce qualitatively different degradation trajectories under recursive generation: temperature sampling accelerates convergence to a low-entropy attractor while top-$k$ attenuates the depth signal, effects beyond single-pass text quality.
```

</details>
